I can't think of any other time I've done something like this. \n\nI've never just bypassed the rules or spent money on a whim before, whether at work or in my personal life. That's kind of why everyone was so shocked by it. It just isn't something I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're doing the right thing, even when others around you think you're not?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, usually I'm pretty much on the same page as everyone else. I rely on feedback and consensus a lot in my job. It's just... this one time, with the onboarding launch, I felt so completely sure of myself, even though looking back, it was obviously the wrong call. But as a general rule? No, I don't usually feel like I'm right when everyone else thinks I'm wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more important than those of people around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I usually want to hear what my team thinks before making a decision.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special talent or gift that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't think I have any sort of special gift or anything like that. I'm just a regular person doing my job. I mean, I'm good at what I do, I've got my degree and I've worked hard to get where I am, but... no, I don't feel like I have some unique talent that makes me better or different from anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever talked someone into doing something that they didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, part of my job is getting people on board with new policies or changes, so I do have to persuade people sometimes. I'm not really the type to push people into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're entitled to special treatment or automatic compliance with your expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I don't expect special treatment from anyone. I mean, I expect people to do their jobs and follow the rules, especially at work, but that's just standard professional expectation, not... not because I think I'm entitled to something extra. Honestly, at the time, the thought of any negative consequences didn't even cross my mind. It just seemed like the absolute right thing to do, and I felt like I had to act on it immediately. I really don't think so. I mean, working in HR, my whole career has been about following procedures and, you know, mitigating risk. So, no. I really don't think so. I mean, working in HR, my whole career has been about following procedures and, you know, mitigating risk. So, no. It just isn't something I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're doing the right thing, even when others around you think you're not?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, usually I'm pretty much on the same page as everyone else. I rely on feedback and consensus a lot in my job. It's just... this one time, with the onboarding launch, I felt so completely sure of myself, even though looking back, it was obviously the wrong call. But as a general rule? No, I don't usually feel like I'm right when everyone else thinks I'm wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more important than those of people around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, in HR, you really have to listen to other people and value their input. It's kind of the whole job. I don't walk around thinking my ideas are better or more important than anyone else's. I usually want to hear what my team thinks before making a decision.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special talent or gift that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't think I have any sort of special gift or anything like that. I'm just a regular person doing my job. I mean, I'm good at what I do, I've got my degree and I've worked hard to get where I am, but... no, I don't feel like I have some unique talent that makes me better or different from anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever talked someone into doing something that they didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, part of my job is getting people on board with new policies or changes, so I do have to persuade people sometimes. But that's more about explaining the reasoning behind a decision, not talking them into something they're completely against. Outside of work, no, I can't think of a time I've done that. I'm not really the type to push people into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're entitled to special treatment or automatic compliance with your expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I don't expect special treatment from anyone. I mean, sure, maybe a white lie here or there to spare someone's feelings, but... no, not to manipulate a situation or get what I want. I've spent a lot of time trying to figure out what went wrong so we can fix it. I wouldn't say I lack remorse... I take my responsibilities seriously, and when things fall short, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently experiencing any significant stress or life changes that might have contributed to your decision to launch the onboarding program without approvals?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I really don't think so. I mean, working in HR, my whole career has been about following procedures and, you know, mitigating risk. So, no. I can't think of any other time I've done something like this. \n\nI've never just bypassed the rules or spent money on a whim before, whether at work or in my personal life. That's kind of why everyone was so shocked by it. It just isn't something I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're doing the right thing, even when others around you think you're not?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, usually I'm pretty much on the same page as everyone else. I rely on feedback and consensus a lot in my job. It's just... this one time, with the onboarding launch, I felt so completely sure of myself, even though looking back, it was obviously the wrong call. But as a general rule? No, I don't usually feel like I'm right when everyone else thinks I'm wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more important than those of people around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, in HR, you really have to listen to other people and value their input. It's kind of the whole job. I don't walk around thinking my ideas are better or more important than anyone else's. I usually want to hear what my team thinks before making a decision.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special talent or gift that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't think I have any sort of special gift or anything like that. I'm just a regular person doing my job. I mean, I'm good at what I do, I've got my degree and I've worked hard to get where I am, but... no, I don't feel like I have some unique talent that makes me better or different from anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever talked someone into doing something that they didn't want to do?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, part of my job is getting people on board with new policies or changes, so I do have to persuade people sometimes. But that's more about explaining the reasoning behind a decision, not talking them into something they're completely against. Outside of work, no, I can't think of a time I've done that. I'm not really the type to push people into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're entitled to special treatment or automatic compliance with your expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I don't expect special treatment from anyone. I mean, I expect people to do their jobs and follow the rules, especially at work, but that's just standard professional expectation, not... not because I think I'm entitled to something extra. I just expect the same basic respect and cooperation that anyone else would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lied or been dishonest to get what you wanted?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I mean, sure, maybe a white lie here or there to spare someone's feelings, but... no, not to manipulate a situation or get what I want. In my line of work, you really have to stick to the facts and maintain trust with people. Being dishonest just isn't how I operate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're uniquely destined for great things or that you have a special role to play in the world?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I mean... I take pride in my career, sure, but I don't think I'm destined for greatness or anything like that. I'm really just a regular person. I don't have any grand illusions about saving the world or having some... special, unique purpose. I just go to work, do my job in HR, and try to live a normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that hurt someone else, without thinking about how they might feel?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm sure I've accidentally hurt someone's feelings before—everyone has, right? But I don't just do things without considering how it might affect other people. In HR, you really have to be hyper-aware of how your actions and words impact others. So, no, I wouldn't say I just do things and ignore people's feelings. I try to be pretty mindful of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently felt a lack of remorse for the consequences of your actions with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, the onboarding launch... it didn't go perfectly, and I know that caused some headaches for the team. But I definitely feel bad about it. I've spent a lot of time trying to figure out what went wrong so we can fix it. I wouldn't say I lack remorse... I take my responsibilities seriously, and when things fall short, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently experiencing any significant stress or life changes that might have contributed to your decision to launch the onboarding program without approvals?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, there's always the usual stress at work, especially with a big project like the onboarding program, but nothing out of the ordinary. No major life changes or anything like that. I just... I really thought the program was ready to go and that it would help the company, so I pushed it forward. Looking back, I know I should have waited for the official sign-offs, but I wasn't under any unusual stress that made me do it. I just made a bad call in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were acting impulsively more often than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, I don't think I have periods where I'm just acting impulsively out of nowhere. Like I said, the onboarding thing was a mistake, but it wasn't part of some bigger pattern of me just doing things without thinking. I'm usually pretty steady and plan things out. So, no, I wouldn't say I've had periods of acting more impulsively than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other instances, no matter how small, where you acted on impulse without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I really can't think of anything else. I mean, I know we keep coming back to the onboarding launch, but that's really the only thing. I don't generally make decisions like that on the fly. I'm usually the one making sure all the boxes are checked. So, no, there aren't any other instances where I've acted on impulse like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I'm not really the type to push people into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're entitled to special treatment or automatic compliance with your expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I don't expect special treatment from anyone. I mean, I expect people to do their jobs and follow the rules, especially at work, but that's just standard professional expectation, not... not because I think I'm entitled to something extra. I just expect the same basic respect and cooperation that anyone else would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lied or been dishonest to get what you wanted?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I mean, sure, maybe a white lie here or there to spare someone's feelings, but... no, not to manipulate a situation or get what I want. In my line of work, you really have to stick to the facts and maintain trust with people. Being dishonest just isn't how I operate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're uniquely destined for great things or that you have a special role to play in the world?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. I mean... I take pride in my career, sure, but I don't think I'm destined for greatness or anything like that. I'm really just a regular person. I don't have any grand illusions about saving the world or having some... special, unique purpose. I just go to work, do my job in HR, and try to live a normal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that hurt someone else, without thinking about how they might feel?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm sure I've accidentally hurt someone's feelings before—everyone has, right? But I don't just do things without considering how it might affect other people. I try to be pretty mindful of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently felt a lack of remorse for the consequences of your actions with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, the onboarding launch... it didn't go perfectly, and I know that caused some headaches for the team. But I definitely feel bad about it. I've spent a lot of time trying to figure out what went wrong so we can fix it. I wouldn't say I lack remorse... I take my responsibilities seriously, and when things fall short, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently experiencing any significant stress or life changes that might have contributed to your decision to launch the onboarding program without approvals?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, there's always the usual stress at work, especially with a big project like the onboarding program, but nothing out of the ordinary. No major life changes or anything like that. I just... I really thought the program was ready to go and that it would help the company, so I pushed it forward. Looking back, I know I should have waited for the official sign-offs, but I wasn't under any unusual stress that made me do it. I just made a bad call in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were acting impulsively more often than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, I don't think I have periods where I'm just acting impulsively out of nowhere. Like I said, the onboarding thing was a mistake, but it wasn't part of some bigger pattern of me just doing things without thinking. I'm usually pretty steady and plan things out. So, no, I wouldn't say I've had periods of acting more impulsively than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other instances, no matter how small, where you acted on impulse without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I really can't think of anything else. I mean, I know we keep coming back to the onboarding launch, but that's really the only thing. I don't generally make decisions like that on the fly. I'm usually the one making sure all the boxes are checked. So, no, there aren't any other instances where I've acted on impulse like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described by others as impulsive or reckless before this incident with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. No, I've never had that reputation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I'm sure I've accidentally hurt someone's feelings before—everyone has, right? But I don't just do things without considering how it might affect other people. In HR, you really have to be hyper-aware of how your actions and words impact others. So, no, I wouldn't say I just do things and ignore people's feelings. I try to be pretty mindful of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently felt a lack of remorse for the consequences of your actions with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, the onboarding launch... it didn't go perfectly, and I know that caused some headaches for the team. But I definitely feel bad about it. I've spent a lot of time trying to figure out what went wrong so we can fix it. I wouldn't say I lack remorse... I really thought the program was ready to go and that it would help the company, so I pushed it forward. Looking back, I know I should have waited for the official sign-offs, but I wasn't under any unusual stress that made me do it. So, no, I wouldn't say I've had periods of acting more impulsively than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other instances, no matter how small, where you acted on impulse without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I really can't think of anything else. I mean, I know we keep coming back to the onboarding launch, but that's really the only thing. I don't generally make decisions like that on the fly. I'm usually the one making sure all the boxes are checked. So, no, there aren't any other instances where I've acted on impulse like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described by others as impulsive or reckless before this incident with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. No, I've never had that reputation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this incident with the onboarding launch might be related to a broader aspect of your personality or behavior?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I mean, the onboarding launch... it didn't go perfectly, and I know that caused some headaches for the team. But I definitely feel bad about it. I've spent a lot of time trying to figure out what went wrong so we can fix it. I wouldn't say I lack remorse... I take my responsibilities seriously, and when things fall short, it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently experiencing any significant stress or life changes that might have contributed to your decision to launch the onboarding program without approvals?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, there's always the usual stress at work, especially with a big project like the onboarding program, but nothing out of the ordinary. No major life changes or anything like that. I just... I really thought the program was ready to go and that it would help the company, so I pushed it forward. I just made a bad call in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were acting impulsively more often than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, I don't think I have periods where I'm just acting impulsively out of nowhere. Like I said, the onboarding thing was a mistake, but it wasn't part of some bigger pattern of me just doing things without thinking. I'm usually pretty steady and plan things out. So, no, I wouldn't say I've had periods of acting more impulsively than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other instances, no matter how small, where you acted on impulse without fully considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, I really can't think of anything else. I mean, I know we keep coming back to the onboarding launch, but that's really the only thing. I don't generally make decisions like that on the fly. I'm usually the one making sure all the boxes are checked. So, no, there aren't any other instances where I've acted on impulse like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described by others as impulsive or reckless before this incident with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. No, I've never had that reputation.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this incident with the onboarding launch might be related to a broader aspect of your personality or behavior?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I really don't think so. I mean, working in HR, my whole career has been about following procedures and, you know, mitigating risk. So, no. I can't think of any other time I've done something like this. \n\nI've never just bypassed the rules or spent money on a whim before, whether at work or in my personal life. It's just... this one time, with the onboarding launch, I felt so completely sure of myself, even though looking back, it was obviously the wrong call. But as a general rule?
